Electron phonon coupling in the topological heavy fermion model of twisted bilayer graphene
Yi-Jie Wang, Geng-Dong Zhou, Biao Lian, Zhi-Da Song

Abstract
On flat bands of the magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene, exotic correlation physics unfolds. Phonons, through mediating an effective electron-electron interaction, can play a crucial role in selecting various electronic phases. In this study, we derive the full electron-phonon coupling (EPC) vertex from the microscopic tight-binding lattice, and identify the significance of each phonon mode. We then project the EPC vertices onto the topological heavy fermion (THF) basis [Song and Bernevig, Phys. Rev. Lett. 129, 047601 (2022)], and show that an anti-Hund’s interaction H ^ A \hat{H}_{\rm A} is induced on each moiré-scale local f f -orbital, with strengths 1 to 4 meV. We analyze the phonon-induced multiplet splittings, which can significantly affect the local correlation. As an example, we elaborate on the phonon-favored symmetry-breaking orders at even-integer fillings. Through systematic self-consistent Hartree-Fock calculations, we uncover a tight competition between Γ \Gamma -phonon-favored orbital orders, K K -phonon-favored inter-valley coherent orders, and the kinetic and Coulomb-favored orders. Contrary to EPC, the carbon atom Hubbard repulsion induces an on- f f -site Hund’s interaction H ^ H \hat{H}_{\rm H} with strengths 1 to 3 meV that partly counteracts the effect of H ^ A \hat{H}_{\rm A} . The combined influence of H ^ A , H \hat{H}_{\rm A,H} on the multiplet splitting and symmetry-breaking states is discussed. In the end, we explore the possibility of finding an exotic Dirac semi-metal formed solely by c c -electrons at the charge-neutrality point, while f f -impurities exhibit a symmetric Mott gap by forming non-degenerate singlets under H ^ A , H \hat{H}_{\rm A,H} . Experimental features that distinguish such a state are discussed.



I
Introduction

Flat band electrons in the magic-angle twisted bilayer graphene (MATBG)
[
1
]
exhibit rich interaction-driven physics.
At integer fillings, correlated symmetry-breaking orders are observed
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
6
]
, which can spontaneously break the
C_{3z}
rotation symmetry
[
7
,
8
]
, or exhibit a Kekulé pattern indicating inter-valley coherence
[
9
]
.
In the presence of external magnetic fields, correlated Chern insulators can be stabilized
[
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
,
16
]
.
With aligned hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) substrate which breaks
C_{2z}T
symmetry, intrinsic Chern insulators are observed
[
17
]
.
At non-integer fillings, the zero-energy peaks and Coulomb blockade observed in the tunneling spectrum
[
18
,
19
,
10
,
20
,
21
]
, as well as the Pomeranchuk effect
[
22
,
23
]
and the sharp compressibility jumps with increased filling
[
18
,
19
,
22
,
23
,
15
]
, together hint at the formation of heavy Fermi liquid.
A superconducting (SC) phase can also develop
[
24
,
3
,
4
,
25
,
11
,
26
,
27
]
, enjoying unconventional features including the proximity to the correlated insulating states in the phase diagram, short coherence length
[
24
,
3
]
, a V-shaped tunneling gap
[
21
]
, and nematicity
[
28
]
.
Suppressing the correlated insulators and superconductors, either by heating up or by external tuning such as enhancing screening or applying magnetic fields, a strange metal with near Planckian dissipation appears
[
29
,
30
,
31
]
, featuring in the large linear-in-
T
resistivity with a wide temperature span.

Due to the non-trivial band topology protected by
C_{2z}T
and an emergent particle-hole symmetry
P

[
32
,
33
,
34
,
35
,
36
]
, finding a lattice representation with a finite orbital number is prohibited, and a theoretical diving into the strongly correlated physics has hence been hindered.
Recently, a topological heavy fermion (THF) model
[
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
]
reveals that, the topological flat bands can be disentangled as an emergent set of moiré-scale local orbitals (
f
-orbitals), which are themselves trivially flat with vanishing inter-site overlaps, hybridized with itinerant Dirac fermions (
c
-electrons), which brings about the non-trivial topology.
The
f
-orbitals dominate the flat band wave-functions in the vast majority of the moiré Brillouin zone (MBZ), while the hybridization with
c
-electrons only occurs in a small vicinity of the
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}
-point.
Within this picture, a large Hubbard-type on-
f
-site repulsion
U\sim 58\mathrm{meV}
, which originates from the long-range Coulomb repulsion between 2D continuum electrons, is identified as the governing energy scale for the flat band electrons. As
U
acts to suppress the
f
-charge fluctuation, the occupation number at each site will tend to some integer
N_{f}
, so that the multiplet of
N_{f}
-electron states behaves as a local moment, coupled to the bath of
c
-electrons.
The strong correlation physics are thus able to be understood in a unified framework
[
41
,
42
,
43
,
44
,
45
,
46
,
47
,
48
,
49
]
:
at non-integer fillings, the
f
-moments are Kondo screened at low temperatures, giving rise to the heavy Fermi liquid behaviors; while at integer fillings, the Ruderman-Kittel-Kasuya-Yosida (RKKY) interaction between
f
-moments, mediated by the
c
-bath, can win over the Kondo screening and lead to symmetry-breaking correlated states.

Interestingly, with only the Coulomb repulsion of 2D electron gas taken into consideration, the flat bands enjoy a rather high emergent
\rm U(4)
symmetry
[
50
,
51
,
52
,
53
,
54
,
37
]
. Numerous correlated phases with tiny energy differences are thus found closely competing; and consequently, any other sources of interaction that splits the orbital, valley or spin configurations, despite weak when compared to
U
, can play a significant role in finally determining the actual ground state.
Especially, electron-phonon couplings (EPC) can be of great importance.
Recent experiments have revealed a prominent EPC between the flat band electrons to the
K
-phonons
[
55
]
, which are then theoretically found to favor inter-valley coherent orders that are symmetric under the spinless-time-reversal
T
at even-integer fillings
[
56
]
.
Such orders include one that preserves the moiré translation (dubbed as
\rm TIVC
) and appears at ultra-low strain, and one that breaks it (dubbed as the incommensurate Kekulé spiral order, IKS) and appears in strained samples
[
57
]
.
These orders match the Kekulé pattern
[
58
]
observed experimentally for the correlated insulators at
\nu=\pm 2

[
9
]
, where
\nu
counts the total electron filling measured from the charge neutrality point (CNP).
In particular,
\rm TIVC
will not win as the ground state in the absence of EPC due to penalization from Coulomb interactions
[
50
,
52
,
53
,
51
,
59
,
60
]
.
Moreover, as experimental evidence accumulates that screening Coulomb repulsion could stabilize the SC phase
[
11
,
26
,
27
]
, phonons appear as a plausible candidate for the pairing glue
[
61
,
62
,
63
,
64
,
65
,
66
,
67
]
.
However, since any phonon-mediated interaction will be far weaker than the large repulsive
U
,
f
-electrons cannot be superconducting in a naïve mean-field picture.
Nevertheless, in the heavy Fermi liquid regime, by splitting the
N_{f}
-electron multiplet, microscopic interactions such as EPC can significantly reshape the Kondo physics
[
68
]
, which then strongly renormalizes the effective interaction among heavy quasi-particles
[
69
,
70
,
71
,
72
,
73
]
.
In a recent work
[
74
]
, under the assumption that the Kondo screening is dominated by a
d
-wave two-electron doublet at
\nu\approx-2.5
, we obtained the asymptotic behavior of the renormalized interaction, and showed that the pairing strength to an inter-valley
d
-wave spin-singlet channel can be attractive.
Such an SC order lies in the strongly correlated regime, which is set by the large
U
and characterized by the small quasi-particle weight, and belongs to the strong coupling (BEC) limit because the pairing strength is of the same order as the heavy quasi-particle kinetic energy. It is also nematic and Euler-obstructed
[
75
,
76
]
, leading to pairing nodes in the momentum space. These results provide a potential explanation to the unconventional SC features observed in experiments that are mentioned above.
Following this line, whether other novel correlated states owing to phonons exist beneath the unexplained experimental findings can be an exciting open question.

For reference of any future study that are interested in these topics, in this article, we present a detailed analysis on the EPC in the THF model.
In
Section

II
, for self-consistency of the article, we shortly review both the continuum model (
Section

II.1
) and the THF model (
Section

II.2
) for electrons.
In
Section

III
, we discuss the phonon motion (
Section

III.1
), and the EPC vertex for moiré electrons in MATBG, which is derived from the tight-binding carbon atom lattice (
Section

III.2
).
The MATBG
\Gamma
-phonon modes are labeled with
(c/r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA}/\mathrm{ZA}/\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO}/\mathrm{ZO})
, where
\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA}/\mathrm{ZA}/\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO}/\mathrm{ZO}
stand for single-layer graphene (SLG) acoustic (
\rm A
) and optical (
\rm O
) motion along
\rm X/Y/Z
directions, and
c/r
stand for layer co-moving and relative-moving. The MATBG
K
-phonons are labeled with
(l,\mathrm{A1}/\mathrm{B1})
, where
l=1,2
denotes layer, and
\mathrm{A1}/\mathrm{B1}
denote two orthogonal SLG phonon modes that have
\sqrt{3}\times\sqrt{3}
pattern, and transform as the
A_{1}
and
B_{1}
irreducible representations (irreps) under the point group
D_{6}
, respectively.
We find that, the out-of-plane
\Gamma
-phonon modes
(c,\mathrm{ZA})
and
(c/r,\mathrm{ZO})
, and all the other
K
-phonons that are not mentioned above, are not significantly coupled to moiré electrons captured in the continuum model.
In the end of
Section

III.2
, we also discuss the renormalization (RG) effect from high-energy electron states (with energies above all phonon-frequencies,
e.g.

\gtrsim
200meV).
In Ref.
[
77
]
, it is found that such RG processes are able to amplify the
K
-phonon-mediated interaction strengths by a factor of
\kappa_{K}=3.2
, from the bare values derived from the tight-binding approach.

In
Section

IV
, we project the EPC vertex onto the
f
-orbitals, which form the major constitute of flat bands, and carry the strong correlation.
We show that all the co-moving in-plane
\Gamma
-phonon modes,
(c,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA}/\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})
, are not coupled to the
f
-orbitals, due to an approximate chiral symmetry
\widetilde{S}
that holds only in the vicinity of AA-stacking regions.
Besides, the layer-breathing
\Gamma
-phonon mode,
(r,\mathrm{ZA})
, also has negligible EPC on the
f
-orbitals, due to crystalline symmetries and the emergent
P
.
The only contributing phonon modes are the relative in-plane
\Gamma
-phonon modes,
(r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA}/\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})
, and
K
-phonons
(l,\mathrm{A1}/\mathrm{B1})
, whose projected EPC vertices are obtained.
Viewing each
f
-orbital as a giant molecule, the EPC vertices induce the Jahn-Teller effect, which dynamically lifts the orbital and valley degeneracies
[
66
,
67
]
.

In
Section

V
, by integrating out the phonon fields, we obtain the phonon-mediated effective interaction for electrons on the
f
-orbitals.
The inter-site interactions are negligible due to the locality of electron orbitals and the locality of phonon fields.
The on-site interaction, which we denote by
\hat{H}_{\rm A}
, however, has strengths
1\sim 4
meV, with the RG factor
\kappa_{K}
taken into consideration.
The multiplet splittings induced by
\hat{H}_{\rm A}
is explicitly solved in
Section

VI
.
It is found that, two-electron states with symmetric spatial wave-functions (encoded by the orbital and valley degrees of freedom) are generically more favored in energy, which, by the fermion anti-symmetry, form spin-singlets.
Especially, at
\kappa_{\Gamma}=1
and
\kappa_{K}=3.2
, an
A_{1}
irrep of
D_{6}
group (
s
-wave) is the unique two-electron ground state, while the
E_{2}
irrep (
d
-wave) afore mentioned comes to the second-lowest states.
We remark that the energy ordering of the
A_{1}
and
E_{2}
irreps can eventually be inversed with the carbon atom Hubbard taken into consideration, but we postpone the discussion to
Section

VIII
.
Three and four-electron multiplet splittings are also solved, where the states with the highest total spin are also found to have the highest energy under
\hat{H}_{\rm A}
.
The three-electron ground states are eight-fold degenerate, guaranteed by symmetries, while the four-electron ground state is a non-degenerate spin-singlet forming
A_{1}
irrep.
Above results imply an anti-Hund’s nature of
\hat{H}_{\rm A}

[
78
,
66
,
67
,
74
]
, which unveils a striking analogue to the trivalent fullerides
\rm A_{3}C_{60}

[
79
,
80
,
81
,
82
]
. There, the Jahn-Teller effect on each
\rm C_{60}
molecule also generates an anti-Hund’s on-site interaction, which eventually leads to spin-singlet SC in the strongly correlated regime.

As an immediate implication of the phonon-mediated
\hat{H}_{\rm A}
, a self-consistent Hartree-Fock study is performed in
Section

VII
to reveal the phonon-favored symmetry-breaking states at even-integer fillings.
In general,
\Gamma
-phonons favor intra-valley orbital orders, including an orbital polarized (
\rm OP
) order, which is a valley Hall insulator, and a nematic inter-orbital coherent Dirac semi-metal (
\rm NIOC
).

K
-phonons favor inter-valley coherent orders, including
\rm TIVC
and another nematic inter-valley coherent state (
\rm NIVC
).
To dissect the roles of distinct phonon modes, we also introduce an overall amplification factor for the
\Gamma
-phonon-mediated interactions,
\kappa_{\Gamma}
. Viewing both
\kappa_{\Gamma}
and
\kappa_{K}
as tunable parameters, we map out the phase diagram.

At
\nu=0
, the Kramer’s inter-valley coherent state (
\rm KIVC
) and the valley-polarized state (
\rm VP
) appear at small
\kappa_{\Gamma}\lesssim 1.0
and small
\kappa_{K}\lesssim 1.0
, as they are favored by kinetic and Coulomb energies
[
52
,
59
,
37
]
. Since
\rm KIVC
is penalized by
K
-phonons, and
\rm VP
is penalized by
\Gamma
-phonons, the two phases are separated by a boundary with slope
\frac{\Delta\kappa_{\Gamma}}{\Delta\kappa_{K}}\approx 0.8
that goes slightly below
\kappa_{\Gamma}=\kappa_{K}=0
.

\rm TIVC
appears in a large area satisfying
\kappa_{K}\gtrsim 0.9
and
0.8\kappa_{K}\gtrsim\kappa_{\Gamma}
, which encompasses
\kappa_{\Gamma}=1
and
\kappa_{K}=3.2
. Nevertheless, Kekulé pattern is not experimentally observed at this filling
[
9
]
. The
\rm OP
and
\rm NIOC
orders also appear in the phase diagram but only at weak
\kappa_{K}
and large
\kappa_{\Gamma}
. We briefly discuss how they can further benefit from the effect of hBN substrate and strain, respectively
[
83
,
57
,
84
]
.
Our results are in qualitative agreement with Refs.
[
66
,
67
,
56
,
85
]
, and the quantitative difference can be attributed to different model settings.

At
\nu=-2
, symmetry-breaking orders polarize two degrees of freedom. At small
\kappa_{\Gamma}\lesssim 1.6
and small
\kappa_{K}\lesssim 1.0
, the lowest symmetry-breaking order is dubbed as
\rm(KIVC\times SP)_{tw}
, which can be understood as
\rm KIVC\times SP
(where
\rm SP
denotes spin-polarization) but with a
\pi
relative spin twist in the two valleys.
Such a twisted state is equally favored by kinetic and Coulomb energies as
\rm KIVC\times SP
and
\rm VP\times SP
, but avoids energy penalty from both
\Gamma
- and
K
-phonons.
Increasing
\kappa_{K}
, we find two orders that are degenerate at the Hartree-Fock level,
\rm TIVC\times QAH
and
\rm TIVC\times QSH
, in a large area that includes
\kappa_{\Gamma}=1
and
\kappa_{K}=3.2
, which can account for the Kekulé pattern observed in ultra-low strained samples at this filling
[
9
,
58
]
.
With the
\rm TIVC
component polarized to benefit from
K
-phonons, two Chern bands with opposite Chern numbers
\pm 1
are populated with both spins. Then, a pair of up and down spins is depleted from either the same Chern sector or two opposite Chern sectors, which generates the quantum anomalous Hall (
\rm QAH
) or quantum spin Hall (
\rm QSH
) response, respectively.
The result is also in agreement with Refs.
[
67
,
56
,
85
]
.
Interestingly, when
\kappa_{\Gamma}
and
\kappa_{K}
are both strong (
\kappa_{\Gamma}+\frac{1}{3}\kappa_{K}\gtrsim\frac{8}{3}
), we identify a new nematic phase, which can be dubbed as
\rm NIOC\times NIVC
.
Such a state preserves
C_{2z}T
but spontaneously violates valley U(1), hence can either be a metal with
C_{2z}T
-protected crossings, or annihilate the crossings to form an insulator, depending on parameters.
By simultaneously forming orbital orders and inter-valley coherence, this state benefits from both
\Gamma
-phonons and
K
-phonons, but do not occur at weak EPC strengths due to a large penalty by kinetic and Coulomb energies.
Since the
\rm NIOC
component benefits from an external strain,
\rm NIOC\times NIVC
can potentially explain the nematic symmetry-breaking orders observed in large-strained samples at this filling
[
8
]
.

In
Section

VIII
, we examine a Hund’s interaction on the
f
-orbitals
\hat{H}_{\rm H}
that microscopically originates from the carbon atom Hubbard
U_{0}

[
61
,
86
,
87
,
74
]
.
The strength is also of the order
1\sim 3
meV.
Most crucially, among all the two-electron states, the
A_{1}
singlet is penalized by
\hat{H}_{\rm H}
while the
E_{2}
doublet is almost unaffected. Consequently, with both
U_{0}
and
\kappa_{K}
being large, the
E_{2}
doublet will come to the two-electron ground state, furnishing the necessary condition for the
E_{2}
-doublet-controlled unconventional SC at
\nu\approx-2.5
suggested by Ref.
[
74
]
.
The favored symmetry-breaking orders in the presence of both
\hat{H}_{\rm A}
and
\hat{H}_{\rm H}
are also investigated. While the phase diagram of varying
\kappa_{\Gamma}
and
\kappa_{K}
is enriched, especially with SP phases appearing at weak
\kappa_{\Gamma}
and
\kappa_{K}
, the outcome at
\kappa_{\Gamma}=1
and
\kappa_{K}=3.2
is seldom affected. At CNP and
\nu=-2
,
\rm TIVC
and
\rm TIVC\times QAH/QSH
are found, respectively.

On the other hand, the four-electron ground state still remains the non-degenerate
A_{1}
irrep even with the onset of
\hat{H}_{\rm H}
. In
Section

IX
, we thus discuss a new exotic phase formed by the light Dirac
c
-electrons only, while
f
-electrons, by forming the non-degenerate singlet, are decoupled from the
c
-bath and become invisible in the low-energy regime. No symmetry-breaking occurs in this strongly correlated state, while
f
-electrons exhibit a full Mott gap. We thus term this state as a Mott semi-metal (Mott-SM).
Its stability against Kondo screening and the symmetry-breaking effects mediated by RKKY interactions are discussed. Experimental features that distinguish it from other semi-metallic states at CNP are outlined. We also comment that the doped Mott-SM is unlikely to support the experimentally observed SC due to the light Dirac nature.

II
The electron Hamiltonian and the topological heavy fermion model

Figure 1:
(a) Folding MBZ at commensurate twist angle
\theta
. (b) Illustration of an MUC and the location of an
f
-site.
(c)
\hat{H}_{0}
bands of the THF model.
(Left) without
cf
hybridization,
f
-electrons will be isolated orbitals with vanishing kinetic energy, and
c
-electrons form Dirac cones with quick dispersion.
(Right) with
cf
hybridization turned on, the topological flat bands and the remote band bottoms in the continuum model of MATBG are faithfully reproduced.

For the article to be self-contained, we first review the continuum model for moiré electrons, and summarize the essential of the THF model.

II.1
The continuum model for electron fields

We label the two graphene layers as
l=1,2
, and the two sublattices in each layer as
\alpha=A,B
, so that
\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)}
runs over all carbon atoms that belong to layer
l
and sublattice
\alpha
.
\psi^{\dagger}_{s}(\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)})
creates an electron with spin
s=\uparrow,\downarrow
in the corresponding tight-binding
p_{z}
-orbital.
The low-energy physics involve electrons with momenta close to the graphene valleys, two per layer, which we dub as
\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}
with
\eta=\pm
,

\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},l,\alpha,\eta,s}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{g}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)}}e^{i(\mathbf{k}+\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)})\cdot\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)}}\psi^{\dagger}_{s}(\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)})

(1)

Here,
N_{g}
denotes the number of graphene unit cells per layer, and a high-energy cutoff will be imposed as
|\mathbf{k}|\lesssim\Lambda
, where
\Lambda\ll|\mathbf{K}^{(l)}|
defines a long-wave limit compared to the microscopic lattice.
Due to the small twist angle
\theta\approx 1.05^{\circ}
, the same valley of two layers will be displaced by a small momentum
k_{\theta}=|\mathbf{K}^{(2)}-\mathbf{K}^{(1)}|\approx\theta|\mathbf{K}^{(l)}|
, thus one should choose
\Lambda\gg k_{\theta}
, so as to capture the moiré physics.

The long-wave limit is represented by the continuum fields,

\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{g}\Omega_{g}}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|\lesssim\Lambda}e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\psi^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k},l,\alpha,\eta,s}\ ,

(2)

where
\Omega_{g}
denotes the area of a graphene unit cell.
Compared to
\psi^{\dagger}_{s}(\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)})
,
\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})
has absorbed the fast oscillating phase
e^{i\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}\cdot\mathbf{r}}
within each
\sqrt{3}\times\sqrt{3}
tripled graphene cell as an internal degree of freedom
\eta
.
The continuum model is built by projecting the tight-binding Hamiltonian to the continuum basis
Eq.

2
(
Sections

H.1.1
and
H.2.1
). The hopping amplitude between two
p_{z}
-orbitals spatially separated by a 3D vector
\mathbf{d}
is parametrized in the following Slater-Koster form
[
88
,
89
,
90
]
,

\displaystyle t(\mathbf{d})=V_{\pi}e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{d}|-a_{0}/\sqrt{3}}{r_{0}}}\left[1-\left(\frac{\mathbf{d}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{z}}}{|\mathbf{d}|}\right)^{2}\right]+V_{\sigma}e^{-\frac{|\mathbf{d}|-c_{0}}{r_{0}}}\left(\frac{\mathbf{d}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{z}}}{|\mathbf{d}|}\right)^{2}

(3)

where we take
V_{\pi}\approx-3.1\mathrm{eV}
as the transfer integral of intra-layer nearest-neighbor
\pi
-bonds,
V_{\sigma}\approx 0.48\mathrm{eV}
as the transfer integral for two orbitals vertically aligned in two layers, and
a_{0}=0.246\mathrm{nm}
as the honeycomb lattice constant,
c_{0}=0.334\mathrm{nm}
as the typical vertical separation of two graphene layers,
r_{0}=0.184a_{0}
as the decay length of hopping amplitudes. The projected continuum Hamiltonian is the Bistritzer-MacDonald model
[
1
]
,

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{\eta,s}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{r}\sum_{l,l^{\prime},\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})

(4)

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-iv_{F}\partial_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\eta)}&w_{1}T^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})\\
w_{1}T^{(\eta)\dagger}(\mathbf{r})&-iv_{F}\partial_{\mathbf{r}}\cdot\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\eta)}\\
\end{pmatrix}_{l\alpha;l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}\psi_{l^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})

Here, the large
2\times 2
block structure stands for layer indices
l,l^{\prime}
, and each block is a
2\times 2
matrix representing sublattices
\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}
, decomposed with Pauli matrices
\sigma
. We define
\boldsymbol{\sigma}^{(\eta)}=(\eta\sigma_{x},\sigma_{y})
, and set
\hbar=1
for convenience. The Dirac velocity of each single layer graphene is determined by
v_{F}=-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}a_{0}V_{\pi}\approx 650\mathrm{meV\cdot nm}
.
The overall inter-layer tunneling strength
w_{1}
is determined by Fouriering
Eq.

3
in a 2D horizontal plane parametrized by
\mathbf{d}_{\parallel}
, with fixed height
\mathbf{d}\cdot\hat{\mathbf{z}}=c_{0}
, at wave vector
\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}
,

\displaystyle w_{1}=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{d}_{\parallel}}{\Omega_{g}}~t(\mathbf{d}_{\parallel}+c_{0}\hat{\mathbf{z}})e^{-i\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}\cdot\mathbf{d}_{\parallel}}\approx 0.228V_{\sigma}\approx 110\mathrm{meV}\ .

(5)

Note that, since
Eq.

3
is isotropic in each horizontal plane,
w_{1}
is real-valued and only depends on
|\mathbf{K}^{(l)}|
.
The inter-layer tunneling matrix reads,

\displaystyle T^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{3}e^{i\eta\mathbf{q}_{j}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\begin{pmatrix}u_{0}&e^{-i\eta\frac{2\pi}{3}(j-1)}\\
e^{i\eta\frac{2\pi}{3}(j-1)}&u_{0}\\
\end{pmatrix}

(6)

where
\mathbf{q}_{j}
is defined as in
Fig.

1
, and
u_{0}\approx 0.8
phenomenologically describes the suppression of tunneling amplitudes at AA-stacked regions (centered at
\mathbf{r}=0
) due to lattice corrugation
[
91
,
90
]
.
We also remark that, in obtaining
Eq.

4
,
O(\theta)
corrections in the intra-layer blocks
[
36
]
and non-local tunnelings
[
57
,
39
]
in the inter-layer blocks are neglected.

In the flat bands of
\hat{H}_{0}
(
Eq.

4
), the dominating energy scale is set by the long-range Coulomb repulsion of moiré electrons, modeled by

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\rm C}=\frac{1}{2}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{r}\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{r}^{\prime}:\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{r}):V(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime}):\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{r}^{\prime}):\ ,

(7)

where
:\hat{\rho}(\mathbf{r}):=\sum_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}\left[\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})\psi_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})-\frac{1}{2}\right]
measures the electron density with respect to a uniform charge-neutral background, which corresponds to the average charge density at the continuum scale.
The Coulomb potential
V(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}^{\prime})
is screened by the top and bottom gates that are separated by distance
\xi_{0}
,

\displaystyle V(\mathbf{r})=\frac{e^{2}}{4\pi\epsilon\epsilon_{0}\xi_{0}}\sum_{n=-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{n}}{\sqrt{(\mathbf{r}/\xi_{0})^{2}+n^{2}}}\ ,

(8)

where
e
denotes the electron charge, and
\epsilon_{0}
and
\epsilon
denote the vacuum and relative dielectric constants. Typically, there are
\epsilon\approx 6
and
\xi_{0}\approx 10
nm.

Both
\hat{H}_{0}
and
\hat{H}_{\rm C}
(
Eqs.

4
and
7
) conserve the U(1) charge and SU(2) spin in each valley
\eta
separately, hence we denote the continuous symmetry group as
\rm U(1)^{2}\times SU(2)^{2}
.
The space group of MATBG is
P622
, which is generated by moiré translations
T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}
and spatial rotations
C_{6z}
and
C_{2x}
. In particular, for an arbitrary moiré lattice vector
\Delta\mathbf{R}
(spanned by
\mathbf{a}_{M1,M2}
, see
Fig.

1
(b)),

T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})T^{-1}_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}=e^{-i\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\Delta\mathbf{R}}\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r}+\Delta\mathbf{R})\ ,

(9)

where
\mathbf{K}_{l}
is the moiré Brillouin zone (MBZ) corner defined in Fig.
1
(a).
The phase
e^{-i\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\Delta\mathbf{R}}
appears in
Eq.

9
as a consequence of absorbing the fast oscillation
e^{i\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}\cdot\mathbf{r}}
to
\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})
. For a commensurate stacking angle
\theta
, the moiré translation is represented on the microscopic lattice as
T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}\psi^{\dagger}_{s}(\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)})T^{-1}_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}=\psi^{\dagger}_{s}(\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)}+\Delta\mathbf{R})
, without any extra phase. By identifying
e^{i\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\Delta\mathbf{R}}=e^{i\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}\cdot\Delta\mathbf{R}}
for arbitrary
\Delta\mathbf{R}
(
Fig.

1
(a)),
Eq.

9
can be directly derived from definitions
Eqs.

1
and
2
.
The two valleys are related by a
C_{2z}
rotation or a spinless time-reversal
T
.
Each valley thus enjoys a magnetic space group
P6^{\prime}2^{\prime}2

[
34
]
, generated by
C_{2z}T
,
C_{3z}
,
C_{2x}
, and moiré translations
T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}
.
We introduce the Pauli matrix on the layer, sublattice, and valley indices as
\rho_{\mu}
,
\sigma_{\mu}
and
\tau_{\mu}
with
\mu=0,x,y,z
, respectively, then the symmetry actions are represented as

\displaystyle g\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})g^{-1}=\sum_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}\psi^{\dagger}_{l^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s}(g\mathbf{r})\left[D(g)\right]_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},l\alpha\eta}

(10)

with

\displaystyle D(C_{3z})

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma_{z}\tau_{z}}\ ,

(11)

\displaystyle D(C_{2x})

\displaystyle=\rho_{x}\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}\ ,

(12)

\displaystyle D(C_{2z})

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}\ ,

(13)

\displaystyle D(T)

\displaystyle=\rho_{0}\sigma_{0}\tau_{x}\ .

(14)

Besides crystalline symmetries, an emergent unitary particle-hole symmetry
P
, is also identified
[
34
,
36
]
.
P
inverses the spatial coordinates,
P\mathbf{r}=-\mathbf{r}
, and

\displaystyle D(P)=-i\rho_{y}\sigma_{0}\tau_{0}\ .

(15)

It can be verified that the bilinear
\hat{H}_{0}
(
Eq.

4
) obeys
P\hat{H}_{0}P=-\hat{H}_{0}
, while the quartic
\hat{H}_{\rm C}
(
Eq.

7
) obeys
P\hat{H}_{\rm C}P^{-1}=\hat{H}_{\rm C}
.
One can thus construct a many-body charge conjugation
\mathcal{P}_{c}
, with
P
followed by a
C_{2z}T
action and an exchange between
\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})
and
\psi_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})
.

\mathcal{P}_{c}
commutes with both
\hat{H}_{0}
and
\hat{H}_{\rm C}
, hence the many-body physics of MATBG at
\pm\nu
fillings will be identical.
In the limit of
u_{0}\to 0
,
\hat{H}_{0}
further obeys a chiral symmetry
C
with
C\mathbf{r}=\mathbf{r}
and
D(C)=\sigma_{z}
(hence the name chiral limit), so that
C\hat{H}_{0}C^{-1}=-\hat{H}_{0}
and
C\hat{H}_{\rm C}C^{-1}=\hat{H}_{\rm C}

[
92
]
.

P
and
C
thus combine to an emergent inversion symmetry in this limit
[
93
]
.
In realistic systems,
P
is broken by the omitted
O(\theta)
corrections
[
36
]
and non-local tunnelings
[
57
,
39
]
.

Finally, we remark that, if truncating the Hilbert space to the flat bands, and ignoring the small flat band width,
C_{2z}P
becomes an exact commuting symmetry for the projected Hamiltonian. Since
C_{2z}P
acts locally in the real space, one can combine
C_{2z}P
with the generators of
\rm U(1)^{2}\times SU(2)^{2}
rotations, so as to generate a
\rm U(4)
group. We term it as the flat-
\rm U(4)

[
50
,
51
,
52
,
53
,
54
,
37
]
.

II.2
The topological heavy fermion model

To further zoom in to the flat bands, we exploit the THF model
[
37
,
38
,
39
,
40
]
, which serves as a faithful representation of all single-electron states within an energy window of
\sim 70
meV, which includes the two flat bands and the bottom of four lowest remote bands (see
Fig.

1
(c)).
Two highly localized
f
-orbitals (labeled by
\beta=1,2
) per moiré unit cell (MUC) are constructed per valley
\eta
and per spin
s
, using the Bloch states within the energy window. They significantly overlap with the flat bands in the majority of the MBZ (
96\%
), except in the vicinity of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}
, where they overlap with the remote band bottoms instead, as a consequence of the non-trivial topology. The details of Wannierization can be found in Ref.
[
37
]
.
They form an
E
irrep of the point group
6^{\prime}2^{\prime}2
at Wyckoff position
1a
(namely, the AA-stacked region).
We denote the corresponding creation operator as
f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}
, where
\mathbf{R}
labels the Wannier center.
All the crystalline symmetries (
Eqs.

11
,
12
and
13
), time-reversal
T
(
Eq.

14
), and the emergent particle-hole
P
(
Eq.

15
) act in a closed form on the
f
-orbitals
[
37
]
,

\displaystyle gf^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}g^{-1}=\sum_{\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}f^{\dagger}_{(g\mathbf{R})\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}\left[D^{f}(g)\right]_{\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\beta\eta}

(16)

with

\displaystyle D^{f}(C_{3z})

\displaystyle=e^{i\frac{2\pi}{3}\sigma^{z}\tau^{z}}\ ,

(17)

\displaystyle D^{f}(C_{2x})

\displaystyle=\sigma^{x}\tau^{0}\ ,

(18)

\displaystyle D^{f}(C_{2z})

\displaystyle=\sigma^{x}\tau^{x}\ ,

(19)

\displaystyle D^{f}(T)

\displaystyle=\sigma^{0}\tau^{x}\ ,

(20)

\displaystyle D^{f}(P)

\displaystyle=i\sigma^{z}\tau^{z}\ ,

(21)

where we have introduced the Pauli matrix on the orbital (
\beta
) and valley (
\eta
) indices under the THF basis as
\sigma^{\mu}
and
\tau^{\mu}
, with
\mu=0,x,y,z
.

The Wannier functions
w^{(\eta)}_{l\alpha,\beta}(\mathbf{r})
define the unitary transformation from the continuum basis to the
f
-orbitals,

\displaystyle f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}=\sum_{l,\alpha}e^{-i\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{r}~w^{(\eta)}_{l\alpha,\beta}(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{R})\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})

(22)

The layer and valley dependent phase
e^{-i\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{R}}
in
Eq.

22
is chosen to compensate the similar phase in
Eq.

9
, so that
f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}
transforms under a moiré translation as
T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}^{-1}=f^{\dagger}_{(\mathbf{R}+\Delta\mathbf{R})\beta\eta s}
. The Wannier functions can be well approximated by the following Gaussian form
[
37
,
94
]
. For the
\beta=1
orbital,

\displaystyle w^{(\eta)}_{lA,1}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=\frac{\alpha_{1}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\zeta_{l}\eta}e^{-\frac{\mathbf{r}^{2}}{2\lambda_{1}^{2}}}

(23)

\displaystyle w^{(\eta)}_{lB,1}(\mathbf{r})

\displaystyle=-\zeta_{l}\frac{\alpha_{2}}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\frac{x+i\eta y}{\lambda_{2}^{2}}e^{i\frac{\pi}{4}\zeta_{l}\eta}e^{-\frac{\mathbf{r}^{2}}{2\lambda_{2}^{2}}}

(24)

Here,
\zeta_{l}=[\rho_{z}]_{l,l}
.

\alpha_{1,2}=0.8193,0.5734
control the relative portion of
A,B
sublattices, and in particular, the
\beta=1
orbital comprises of mostly the
A
sublattice.

\lambda_{1,2}=0.1791a_{M},0.1910a_{M}
control the Gaussian width, with
a_{M}\sim\frac{a_{0}}{\theta}
denoting the moiré lattice constant.
The
\beta=2
Wannier function is related to the
\beta=1
Wannier function by conjugating
Eq.

22
with
C_{2z}T
,

\displaystyle w^{(\eta)}_{lA,1}(\mathbf{r})=w^{(\eta)*}_{lB,2}(-\mathbf{r}),\quad w^{(\eta)}_{lB,1}(\mathbf{r})=w^{(\eta)*}_{lA,2}(-\mathbf{r})\ .

(25)

We also note that, the Wannier functions with the same
\beta
but opposite valley
\eta
are related by the time-reversal
T
, hence sharing the same density profile in real space.

With the
f
-components projected out, the remaining Bloch states form four dispersive bands per valley per spin at
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}
, with a quadratic Dirac point in the middle (
Fig.

1
(c)).
They are termed as
c
-electrons.
Due to the fast Dirac velocity, to faithfully represent the low-energy electron states, it suffices to
\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{p}
-expand the
c
-electron Hamiltonian and the
cf
-hybridization in the vicinity of
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}_{M}
.
We denote the corresponding creation operators as
c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}b\eta s}
, with
|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}
and
b=1,2,3,4
. The cutoff
\Lambda_{c}
can be formally extended to infinity without altering the low-energy physics, but in practice, one can take
\Lambda_{c}
as a few times of
k_{\theta}
.
Projecting the kinetic energy
\hat{H}_{0}

Eq.

4
to these THF basis, one obtains

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{M}}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\sum_{b\beta\eta s}e^{-\frac{\lambda^{2}|\mathbf{k}|^{2}}{2}}\left[e^{-i\mathbf{k}\cdot\mathbf{R}}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}b\eta s}H_{b\beta}^{(cf,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})f_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}+\mathrm{H.c.}\right]+\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|<\Lambda_{c}}\sum_{bb^{\prime}\eta s}c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}b\eta s}H_{bb^{\prime}}^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})c_{\mathbf{k}b^{\prime}\eta s}

(26)

\displaystyle H^{(cf,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma\sigma^{0}+v_{\star}^{\prime}(\eta\mathbf{k}_{x}\sigma^{x}+\mathbf{k}_{y}\sigma^{y})\\
0_{2\times 2}\\
\end{pmatrix},\quad H^{(c,\eta)}(\mathbf{k})=\begin{pmatrix}0_{2\times 2}&v_{\star}(\eta\mathbf{k}_{x}\sigma^{0}+i\mathbf{k}_{y}\sigma^{z})\\
v_{\star}(\eta\mathbf{k}_{x}\sigma^{0}-i\mathbf{k}_{y}\sigma^{z})&M\sigma^{x}\\
\end{pmatrix}

Here,
N_{M}\sim\theta^{2}N_{g}
denotes the number of MUC’s,
\lambda=0.3375a_{M}
is a damping factor proportional to the size of
f
-orbitals, and decouples high-energy
c
-electrons from the flat bands. The Dirac velocity of
c
-electrons is found as
v_{\star}=-430.3\mathrm{meV\cdot nm}
.
M=3.697\mathrm{meV}
couples the two Dirac cones to produce a quadratic Dirac point, and
\gamma=-24.75\mathrm{meV}
,
v_{\star}^{\prime}=162.2\mathrm{meV\cdot nm}
describe
cf
-hybridization.

Projecting the long-range Coulomb interaction
Eq.

7
to the THF basis, we obtain

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\rm C}

\displaystyle=\hat{H}_{U}+\hat{H}_{W}+\hat{H}_{J}+\hat{H}_{V}\ ,

(27)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{U}

\displaystyle=\frac{U}{2}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\left[\hat{N}_{f}(\mathbf{R})-4\right]^{2}+\frac{U_{2}}{2}\sum_{\langle\mathbf{R},\mathbf{R}^{\prime}\rangle}\left[\hat{N}_{f}(\mathbf{R})-4\right]\left[\hat{N}_{f}(\mathbf{R}^{\prime})-4\right]\ ,

(28)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{W}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}e^{i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}\left[\hat{N}_{f}(\mathbf{R})-4\right]\left[\sum_{b\eta s}W_{b}:c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}b\eta s}c_{\mathbf{k}b\eta s}:\right]\ ,

(29)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{J}

\displaystyle=-\frac{J}{N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}}\sum_{|\mathbf{k}|,|\mathbf{k}^{\prime}|<\Lambda_{c}}e^{i(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{k}^{\prime})\cdot\mathbf{R}}\sum_{\beta\eta s}\sum_{\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}\frac{\eta\eta^{\prime}+(-1)^{\beta+\beta^{\prime}}}{2}:f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}::c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}(\beta^{\prime}+2)\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}(\beta+2)\eta s}:\ ,

(30)

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{V}

\displaystyle=\frac{V}{2N_{M}}\sum_{\mathbf{k},\mathbf{k}^{\prime},\mathbf{q}}\sum_{b\eta s}\sum_{b^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:c^{\dagger}_{(\mathbf{k}-\mathbf{q})b\eta s}c_{\mathbf{k}b\eta s}::c^{\dagger}_{(\mathbf{k}^{\prime}+\mathbf{q})b^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}b^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:\ .

(31)

Here,
\hat{H}_{U,W,V}
are density-density interactions, and
\hat{H}_{J}
is an exchange interaction between
f
-orbital and
c
-electrons.
\hat{N}_{f}(\mathbf{R})=\sum_{\beta\eta s}f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}
counts the occupation number of an
f
-orbital, and
:f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:=f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}f_{\mathbf{R}\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\beta\eta s,\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}
,
:c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}b\eta s}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}b^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}:=c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}b\eta s}c_{\mathbf{k}^{\prime}b^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}-\frac{1}{2}\delta_{\beta\eta s,\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s^{\prime}}
.
For
\epsilon=6
and
\xi=10\mathrm{nm}
, it is found that
U=57.95\mathrm{meV}
,
U_{2}=2.329\mathrm{meV}
,
W_{1}=W_{2}=44.03\mathrm{meV}
,
W_{3}=W_{4}=50.20\mathrm{meV}
,
J=16.38\mathrm{meV}
,
V=48.33\mathrm{meV}
.
Other interaction terms that does not conserve the
f
-charge number are omitted, as they are related to high-energy excitations that at least cost an energy
\sim U
.

Finally, it can be noted that in the limit of
M\to 0
,
\hat{H}_{0}
takes a bipartite form, namely, regarding
c^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{k}b\eta s}
with
b=1,2
and the rest of electronic fields as two subsystems, only mutual couplings exist. This corresponds to a new unitary chiral symmetry
S
. One thus obtains
C_{2z}PS
as a commuting symmetry for both
\hat{H}_{0}
and
\hat{H}_{\rm C}
, which is local in real space and flips valley. Combining
C_{2z}PS
with the
\rm U(1)^{2}\times SU(2)^{2}
generators, a
\rm U(4)
group is generated. It has been shown that this
\rm U(4)
group is consistent with the flat-U(4) group defined for the continuum model, when projected onto the flat bands
[
37
]
. Surprisingly, the U(4) group in the THF model holds for the entire model beyond the flat bands, without any necessity of projection. We will not distinguish the two groups in below. The flat-U(4) generators projected onto the
f
-orbitals read

\displaystyle\sigma^{0}\tau^{0}s^{\mu},\quad\sigma^{y}\tau^{x}s^{\mu},\quad\sigma^{y}\tau^{y}s^{\mu},\quad\sigma^{z}\tau^{z}s^{\mu}

(32)

with
s^{\mu}
(
\mu=0,x,y,z
) denoting the Pauli matrices for spins. Specially,
\hat{H}_{J}
can be understood as a
ferromagnetic
interaction between the flat-U(4) moment of
f
-electrons and that of
c
-electrons for
b=3,4

[
37
]
.
In the present article, we keep
M
as its original finite value, hence the flat-U(4) symmetry holds only approximately.

III
Electron-phonon couplings for generic moiré electrons

In this section and in
Appendix

H
, we derive the EPC vertices from the microscopic tight-binding lattice, where the phonon vibration alters interatomic distances, hence changes the electron hopping amplitudes. The results are then projected to the continuum model for electrons, which only involves electrons near the valley bottoms.
The derivation follows Ref.
[
90
]
and generalizes to include arbitrary phonon modes, including optical phonons and out-of-plane modes.
For clarity, most of the details are presented in
Appendix

H
.
Here, we only summarize the main results, with brief explanation to the form and strengths of the EPC vertices that we obtain.

Apart from affecting the interatomic hoppings, longitudinal acoustic phonons also modulate the local area, creating a deformation potential that couples to the local electron density
[
95
,
96
,
97
,
98
]
. We analyze the effect of this EPC mechanism in
Appendix

I
, and find that, when projected to the local
f
-orbitals, it only contributes a weak attractive Hubbard interaction that can be absorbed to the large repulsive
\hat{H}_{U}
(
Eq.

28
). It does not induce any multiplet splitting, hence will not be the focus of the present article.

III.1
The continuum model for phonon fields

On an equal footing as electron fields, phonon fields are obtained by quantizing lattice vibration. We denote the displacement vector of the carbon atom located at
\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)}
as
\mathbf{u}_{i}(\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)})
, with
i=x,y,z
. The Fourier component of graphene lattice momentum
\mathbf{q}
reads

\displaystyle\mathbf{u}^{(l,\alpha)}_{\mathbf{q},i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{g}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)}}e^{-i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)}}\mathbf{u}_{i}(\mathbf{R}^{(l,\alpha)})\ .

(33)

Note that
\mathbf{q}
is defined up to the graphene Brillouin zone of each layer (
\mathrm{gBZ}^{(l)}
).
If the two graphene layers are decoupled,
l
and
\mathbf{q}\in\mathrm{gBZ}^{(l)}
will both remain good quantum numbers. For a phonon to affect the low-energy electronic physics, it should scatter an electron in the valley
\eta
either to the same valley, or to the opposite valley
\overline{\eta}
.
Momentum conservation thus implies that the phonon lattice momentum should be either
|\mathbf{q}|\lesssim\Lambda
, or
|\mathbf{q}-\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}|\lesssim\Lambda
(
\eta=\pm
). We will term them as
\Gamma
-phonons and
K
-phonons, respectively. Viewed from the low-energy electrons, how the
\Gamma
-phonon bands and
K
-phonon bands are connected in the graphene Brillouin zone is irrelevant, hence they can be effectively deemed as two independent new species.

Turning on the inter-layer couplings, different
l
and
\mathbf{q}
will hybrid.
Due to the long-wave nature of the moiré couplings, the momentum hybridization should only span several times of
k_{\theta}
, much smaller than
|\mathbf{K}^{(l)}|
, as in the case of moiré electrons. Therefore, the
\Gamma
-phonons and
K
-phonons are still well isolated. We now analyze the two species separately.

For
\Gamma
-phonons,
\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{q},i}^{(l,\alpha)}
can hybrid with
\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{q},i^{\prime}}^{(\overline{l},\alpha^{\prime})}
, as well as
\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{g},i^{\prime}}^{(l^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime})}
, where
\mathbf{g}
denotes a non-zero moiré reciprocal lattice vector (
Fig.

1
(a)).
The former type, referred to as non-moiré couplings, is also present in non-moiré multilayers,
e.g.
, Bernal-stacked bilayer graphene, while the latter type accounts for moiré foldings.
In the present article, we will ignore the moiré foldings of phonons, which can be justified by the weak van der Waals nature of the inter-layer phonon dynamic matrix elements
[
99
]
.
Recovering the moiré foldings can reconstruct the phonon density of states and the phonon wave-functions for an energy scale
\lesssim
1meV
[
100
,
101
,
102
,
103
]
, which is small compared to the characteristic phonon frequency that couples to the moiré electrons.
As we are only interested in the phonon-mediated effective interaction among electrons, which integrates over the full phonon spectrum, such reconstruction should not result in qualitative error. Therefore, we can still label
\Gamma
-phonons with
\mathbf{q}
(
|\mathbf{q}|\lesssim\Lambda
).
The non-moiré couplings, on the other hand, can be expected to be of the same order as in the non-moiré multilayers
[
104
,
100
]
.

We therefore consider the following single-layer modes
\chi_{g}@\Gamma\in\{\mathrm{XA},\mathrm{YA},\mathrm{ZA},\mathrm{ZO},\mathrm{XO},\mathrm{YO}\}
as the complete basis for
\Gamma
-phonons,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u_{\mathbf{q},l,\mathrm{IA}}\\
u_{\mathbf{q},l,\mathrm{IO}}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{q},i}^{(l,A)}\\
\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{q},i}^{(l,B)}\\
\end{pmatrix},

(34)

where
|\mathbf{q}|\lesssim\Lambda
, and
\rm IA,IO
with
\rm I\!=\!X,Y,Z
denote the acoustic (A) or optical (O) vibration along
i\!=\!x,y,z
directions.
In this work, the splitting between the XA and YA motion to the longitudinal and transverse modes will be neglected, and we will take the corresponding sound velocity as
v_{s}=1.7\times 10^{4}\mathrm{m/s}=\frac{3.6\mathrm{meV}}{k_{\theta}}
, which is an average of the original longitudinal (
2.1\times 10^{4}
m/s) and transverse (
1.4\times 10^{4}
m/s) sound velocities in SLG
[
95
,
105
]
. Such an approximation for acoustic modes is expected to be valid for the purpose of estimating the effective interaction, as the splitting will not change the order of magnitude of the induced interaction, which we find weak when compared to the interaction strength from optical modes (
\sim 20\%
, see
Sections

III.2
and
V
)).

To account for the non-moiré inter-layer couplings, it is also convenient to introduce the layer co-moving (
c
) and relative-moving (
r
) basis

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u_{\mathbf{q},c,\chi_{g}@\Gamma}\\
u_{\mathbf{q},r,\chi_{g}@\Gamma}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&-\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_{\mathbf{q},1,\chi_{g}@\Gamma}\\
u_{\mathbf{q},2,\chi_{g}@\Gamma}\\
\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(35)

For the acoustic
\Gamma
-phonons, co-moving modes are not affected by stacking, thus as in SLG, in-plane (
c,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA}
) phonons disperse linearly with
\mathbf{q}
as
\omega_{\mathbf{q},c,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA}}=v_{s}|\mathbf{q}|
, and out-of-plane (
c,\mathrm{ZA}
) phonons disperse quadratically. As will be shown in
Section

III.2
,
(c,\mathrm{ZA})
does not contribute to EPC, hence we will not make further discussion on its dispersion.
For the in-plane relative modes (
r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA}
), in the limit of
\mathbf{q}\to 0
, they can be deemed as a relative glide between two continuum sheets, which simply shifts the moiré pattern hence does not cost energy
[
102
]
. For the microscopic lattice at incommensurate small twist-angle, the same argument also applies.
Therefore,
\omega_{\mathbf{q},r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA}}
should vanish linearly at
\mathbf{q}\to 0
as well. This behavior in MATBG is to be contrasted with that in non-moiré bilayers, where a gap typically
\sim
2meV is opened at
\mathbf{q}=0

[
104
,
100
]
.
Since the inter-layer coupling is weak, the velocity of
(r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})
should also approach that of
(c,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})
for larger
|\mathbf{q}|\lesssim\Lambda

[
104
,
100
,
101
]
, hence we take
\omega_{\mathbf{q},r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA}}=v_{s}|\mathbf{q}|
as well.
For the
(r,\mathrm{ZA})
mode, since it alters the layer spacing, which does affect the total energy, its frequency will have a finite gap at
\mathbf{q}=0
. According to calculations on various bilayer graphenes such as Refs.
[
104
,
100
]
, we set
\omega_{\mathbf{q},r,\mathrm{ZA}}=11\mathrm{meV}
, where the weak dispersion at small finite
\mathbf{q}
is neglected.
For optical
\Gamma
-phonons, as their frequencies are high (all
\gtrsim 100\mathrm{meV}
), we will neglect the small splittings due to inter-layer couplings, as well as the weak dispersions with
\mathbf{q}
, and set
\omega_{\mathbf{q},c/r,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO}}=180\mathrm{meV}
and
\omega_{\mathbf{q},c/r,\mathrm{ZO}}=100\mathrm{meV}

[
104
,
100
,
55
]
.

For
K
-phonons in MATBG, in contrast to the
\Gamma
-phonons, the simple strategy to separate out non-moiré couplings would be invalid. For example, if we naively restrict
\mathbf{u}^{(1,\alpha)}_{\mathbf{K}^{(1)},i}
to be coupled to
\mathbf{u}^{(2,\alpha)}_{\mathbf{K}^{(1)},i^{\prime}}
only, then since
\mathbf{K}^{(1)}
is not a
C_{3z}
-invariant point in
\mathrm{gBZ}^{(2)}
, such an assignment necessarily violates
C_{3z}
symmetry; while if all the
C_{3z}
-related momenta are included, momenta will only be preserved up to MBZ. Nevertheless, since all the
K
-phonon frequencies are high (in-plane
\gtrsim 110\mathrm{meV}
, and out-of-plane
\gtrsim 60\mathrm{meV}
), any small inter-layer couplings and weak dispersions can be safely ignored, too. Consequently, eigen modes can still be labeled by single-layer modes.
Importantly, as will be shown in
Section

III.2
, in the vicinity of each zone corner
\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}
, only one mode per layer will contribute significantly to EPC, with wave-function

\displaystyle u_{\mathbf{q},l,\eta K}

\displaystyle=(-i\eta)\frac{\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{q}+\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)},x}^{(l,A)}+\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{q}+\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)},x}^{(l,B)}}{2}

(36)

\displaystyle-\frac{\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{q}+\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)},y}^{(l,A)}-\mathbf{u}_{\mathbf{q}+\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)},y}^{(l,B)}}{2}\ .

In particular,
u_{0,l,\eta K}
forms an
A_{1}
irrep of the little group
D_{3}
at
\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}
; and superposing the two
A_{1}
irreps at opposite
\eta
induces an
A_{1}
irrep and a
B_{1}
irrep for the SLG point group
D_{6}
.
The corresponding vibration pattern induce EPC by changing the nearest-neighbor
\pi
-bond lengths in the single-layer graphene.
u_{\mathbf{q},l,\eta K}
with small
|\mathbf{q}|\lesssim\Lambda
describes the slow modulation of such vibration.
However, for the same reason as electron fields, the moiré translation will act as
T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}u_{\mathbf{q},l,\eta K}T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}^{-1}=e^{i\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\Delta\mathbf{R}}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\Delta\mathbf{R}}u_{\mathbf{q},l,\eta K}
with an extra
e^{i\eta\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\Delta\mathbf{R}}
phase, which would lead to inconvenience of a continuum description.
To remove this phase, we therefore consider the following
K
-phonon basis (
Section

H.1.3
),

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}u_{\mathbf{q},l,\mathrm{A1}}\\
u_{\mathbf{q},l,\mathrm{B1}}\\
\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\\
\frac{i}{\sqrt{2}}&\frac{-i}{\sqrt{2}}\\
\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}u_{\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{K}_{l},l,+K}\\
u_{\mathbf{q}+\mathbf{K}_{l},l,-K}\\
\end{pmatrix}\ ,

(37)

where by a momentum shift on the right-hand side,
\mathbf{q}
(with
|\mathbf{q}|\lesssim\Lambda
) on the left-hand side is measured from an origin that is equivalent to
\boldsymbol{\Gamma}
under MBZ folding (
Fig.

1
(a)). Accordingly, we define the single-layer
K
-phonon modes as
\chi_{g}@K\in\{\mathrm{A1},\mathrm{B1}\}
, and we set the frequencies as
\omega_{\mathbf{q},l,\mathrm{A1}/\mathrm{B1}}=150\mathrm{meV}

[
104
,
100
,
55
]
.

For notation brevity, we will use
\chi@\Gamma=(c/r,\chi_{g}@\Gamma)
and
\chi@K=(l,\chi_{g}@K)
with
l=1,2
to collectively denote the
\Gamma
-phonon and
K
-phonon modes in MATBG, respectively. If
\chi
or
\chi_{g}
is used alone without specifying
@\Gamma
or
@K
, it includes both
\Gamma
-phonon and
K
-phonon modes.
By definitions in
Eqs.

35
and
37
, both
\Gamma
-phonons and
K
-phonons obey
u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}=u^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},\chi}
, and transform under moiré translation
T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}
as

\displaystyle T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}T^{-1}_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}=e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\Delta\mathbf{R}}u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}\ .

(38)

This allows defining the continuum phonon fields for both species,

\displaystyle u_{\chi}(\mathbf{r})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{g}}}\sum_{|\mathbf{q}|\lesssim\Lambda}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}\ ,

(39)

so that
u_{\chi}(\mathbf{r})
is real-valued, and obeys
T_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}u_{\chi}(\mathbf{r})T^{-1}_{\Delta\mathbf{R}}=u_{\chi}(\mathbf{r}+\Delta\mathbf{R})
.
Phonon fields also obey other crystalline symmetries and time-reversal
T
, which is analyzed in Appendix
A
.

To obtain the phonon propagator, we first convert
u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}
to the Matsubara frequency domain
u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}(iq_{0})
, where
iq_{0}
stands for bosonic Matsubara frequencies. With decomposition

\displaystyle u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}(iq_{0})=\sqrt{\frac{1}{2m_{0}\omega_{\mathbf{q},\chi}}}\left(b_{\mathbf{q},\chi}(iq_{0})+b^{\dagger}_{-\mathbf{q},\chi}(-iq_{0})\right)\ ,

(40)

where
m_{0}
denotes the carbon atom mass, the phonon action reads,

\displaystyle S_{\rm ph}=\sum_{\mathbf{q},\chi}\sum_{q_{0}}b^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\chi}(iq_{0})(-iq_{0}+\omega_{\mathbf{q},\chi})b_{\mathbf{q},\chi}(iq_{0})\ ,

(41)

The phonon propagator is thus given by

\displaystyle D^{(\chi)}(\mathbf{q},iq_{0})

\displaystyle=-\langle u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}(iq_{0})u^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{q},\chi}(iq_{0})\rangle_{\rm ph}

(42)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{m_{0}}\frac{-1}{(\omega_{\mathbf{q},\chi})^{2}+(q_{0})^{2}}\ ,

where
\langle\cdots\rangle_{\rm ph}
stands for the functional average weighted by
e^{-S_{\rm ph}}
.
We will work in the convention that
u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}
instead of
b_{\mathbf{q},\chi}
is chosen as the basic phonon fields, hence our definition of the propagator
Eq.

42
and the EPC vertex
Eq.

43
can be different from some literatures. Nevertheless, the convention is self-consistent, and there should be no ambiguity in the effective interaction (
e.g.

Eqs.

44
and
71
), which is constructed by two EPC vertices and a propagator line.

III.2
Electron-phonon couplings

\chi

(c,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})

(c,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})

(r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})

(r,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})

(c,\mathrm{ZA})

(r,\mathrm{ZA})

(c/r,\mathrm{ZO})

(l,\mathrm{A1}/\mathrm{B1})

\mathcal{V}^{(\chi)}
(meV)

(meV)

(meV)

(meV)

(meV)

1.19 (1.76)

Table 1:
Phonon-mediated interaction strengths obtained at the bare tight-binding level, namely
\kappa_{\Gamma}=\kappa_{K}=1
.
\mathcal{V}^{(\chi)}
is a rough measure of the interaction felt by typical moiré electrons at the AA-stacked region. “-” indicates no contribution. “
<0.01
” indicates extremely weak contribution whose absolute strength does not exceed
0.01\mathrm{meV}
.
“
\infty
” indicates the artificial result that
\mathcal{V}^{(\chi)}
cannot distinguish the inter-energy-band scatterings from intra-energy-band scatterings, which are finite as discussed in the main text.

J_{\rm a,b,c,d,e}
are the phonon-mediated anti-Hund’s on-
f
-site interaction parameters. Values estimated from the numeric Wannier functions (no brackets) and from the Gaussian fit (in brackets) are both presented. In particular,
J_{\rm a,b,d,e}
are multiplet splitting parameters, while
J_{\rm c}
simply indicates a Hubbard-type attraction that can be absorbed to
U
.
Due to the particle-hole symmetry
P
of the
f
-orbital Wannier functions,
J_{\rm c}=0
for
(r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})
and
(r,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})
.

According to
Eq.

3
, when the interatomic distance
\mathbf{d}
is changed under lattice vibration, the hopping amplitude
t(\mathbf{d})
will be altered, and the (bare) EPC vertex can be derived, with the EPC strengths determined by the tight-binding parameters.
Leaving the derivation details to
Appendix

H
, we summarize the results here with sketchy explanations.

In general, the EPC vertex in the continuum model takes the following form

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{epc}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{g}}}\sum_{s}\sum_{\chi}\sum_{|\mathbf{q}|\lesssim\Lambda}\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{r}~u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}\sum_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime},l,l^{\prime},\eta,\eta^{\prime}}

(43)

\displaystyle\psi^{\dagger}_{l,\alpha,\eta,s}(\mathbf{r})C^{(\chi)}_{l\alpha\eta,l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})\psi_{l^{\prime},\alpha^{\prime},\eta^{\prime},s}(\mathbf{r})e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}

C^{(\chi)}_{l\alpha\eta,l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})
can be understood as a
\mathbf{q}
- and
\mathbf{r}
-dependent tensor with one phonon index (
\chi
), and two electron indices (
l\alpha\eta
and
l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}
).
C^{(\chi)}
with given
\chi
,
\mathbf{q}
, and
\mathbf{r}
is hence an
8\times 8
matrix that can be decomposed with layer (
\rho
), sublattice (
\sigma
) and valley (
\tau
) Pauli matrices.
The Hermiticity of
\hat{H}_{\rm epc}
dictates that
C^{(\chi)}(\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})=C^{(\chi)\dagger}(-\mathbf{q},\mathbf{r})
.

In order to estimate the relevance of each phonon mode
\chi
in the continuum model, we introduce

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}^{(\chi)}=\frac{\theta^{2}}{m_{0}}\lim_{\mathbf{q}\to 0}\frac{||C^{(\chi)}(\mathbf{q};0)\otimes C^{(\chi)}(-\mathbf{q};0)||}{\omega_{\mathbf{q},\chi}^{2}}

(44)

as a rough measure of the zero-frequency interaction strength felt by a typical moiré electron at
\mathbf{r}=0
. Here,
\frac{1}{m_{0}}\frac{1}{\omega^{2}_{\mathbf{q},\chi}}=-D^{(\chi)}(\mathbf{q},0)
originates from the zero-frequency phonon propagator, and
||\cdots||
denotes the Frobenius norm.
The
\theta^{2}\sim\frac{\Omega_{g}}{\Omega_{M}}
factor is introduced for the expanded MUC size
\Omega_{M}
with respect to the microscopic graphene unit cells
\Omega_{g}
, and hence the lowered characteristic energy scale.

We start with
\Gamma
-phonons.
(c,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})
modulate the graphene
\pi
-bond lengths in a long-wave manner, resulting in intra-layer electron scatterings with amplitudes
\sim\frac{v_{F}|\mathbf{q}|}{r_{0}}
, while leaving the moiré pattern and hence the inter-layer tunnelings unchanged. Correspondingly, it can be derived that

\displaystyle C^{(c,\mathrm{XA})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{v_{F}}{4r_{0}}\cdot(-iq_{x}\rho_{0}\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}+iq_{y}\rho_{0}\sigma_{y}\tau_{z}),

(45)

\displaystyle C^{(c,\mathrm{YA})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{v_{F}}{4r_{0}}\cdot(iq_{x}\rho_{0}\sigma_{y}\tau_{z}+iq_{y}\rho_{0}\sigma_{x}\tau_{0})

(46)

It can thus be estimated that
\mathcal{V}^{(c,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})}=\frac{\theta^{2}}{m_{0}r_{0}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\frac{v_{F}}{v_{s}}\right)^{2}\sim 0.96\mathrm{meV}
.
As for
(c,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})
, they vibrate the
\pi
-bonds within each microscopic unit cell, leading to intra-layer scatterings

\displaystyle C^{(c,\mathrm{XO})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{3}v_{F}}{a_{0}r_{0}}\cdot\rho_{0}\sigma_{y}\tau_{z}\ ,

(47)

\displaystyle C^{(c,\mathrm{YO})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{3}v_{F}}{a_{0}r_{0}}\cdot\rho_{0}(-\sigma_{x})\tau_{0}\ .

(48)

In principle,
(c,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})
can also wobble the opposite sublattice of opposite layers, inducing inter-layer scatterings.
The corresponding amplitude (derived in
Section

H.2.2
) can be quickly estimated as follows. For the inter-layer tunneling
Eq.

5
, if sublattice
\alpha
of layer 1 is shifted rigidly by a horizontal vector
\mathbf{u}
relative to the sublattice
\alpha^{\prime}
of layer 2, then substituting
t(\mathbf{d}_{\parallel}+c_{0}\hat{\mathbf{z}})\to t(\mathbf{d}_{\parallel}+\mathbf{u}+c_{0}\hat{\mathbf{z}})
generates an additional phase
e^{i\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}\cdot\mathbf{u}}
to
w_{1}
, which when expanded to the linear order of
\mathbf{u}
results in EPC strength proportional to
w_{1}|\mathbf{K}^{(l)}|
.
Since
w_{1}|\mathbf{K}^{(l)}|\sim\frac{1}{30}\frac{v_{F}}{r_{0}a_{0}}
, the inter-layer processes will be negligible compared to the intra-layer processes. Similar analysis also applies to the relative in-plane optical modes,
(r,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})
, and

\displaystyle C^{(r,\mathrm{XO})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{3}v_{F}}{a_{0}r_{0}}\cdot\rho_{z}\sigma_{y}\tau_{z}\ ,

(49)

\displaystyle C^{(r,\mathrm{YO})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{3}v_{F}}{a_{0}r_{0}}\cdot\rho_{z}(-\sigma_{x})\tau_{0}\ .

(50)

(c,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})
and
(r,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})
are thus of the same significance for generic moiré electrons captured by the continuum model, with
\mathcal{V}^{(c,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})}=\mathcal{V}^{(r,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})}=\frac{\theta^{2}}{m_{0}r_{0}^{2}}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{6}v_{F}/a_{0}}{\omega_{0,c,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO}}}\right)^{2}\sim 2.96\mathrm{meV}
.

For the relative glide
(r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})
, however, aside from inducing intra-layer scatterings as
(c,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})
, they also shift the moiré pattern horizontally, with the amplitude magnified by a factor of
\frac{1}{\theta}

[
63
]
, leading to

\displaystyle C^{(r,\mathrm{XA})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{v_{F}}{4r_{0}}(-iq_{x}\rho_{z}\sigma_{x}\tau_{0}+iq_{y}\rho_{z}\sigma_{y}\tau_{z})-\frac{w_{1}}{\theta}\partial_{y}T(\mathbf{r})

(51)

\displaystyle C^{(r,\mathrm{YA})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{v_{F}}{4r_{0}}(iq_{x}\rho_{z}\sigma_{y}\tau_{z}+iq_{y}\rho_{z}\sigma_{x}\tau_{0})+\frac{w_{1}}{\theta}\partial_{x}T(\mathbf{r})

(52)

where
T(\mathbf{r})
denotes the layer-off-diagonal part in the first-quantized matrix of
\hat{H}_{0}
, defined by
[T(\mathbf{r})]_{1\alpha\eta,2\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}=\delta_{\eta,\eta^{\prime}}[T^{(\eta)}(\mathbf{r})]_{\alpha,\alpha^{\prime}}
, and
[T(\mathbf{r})]_{2\alpha\eta,1\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}=[T(\mathbf{r})]^{*}_{1\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},2\alpha\eta}
.
In contrast with intra-layer scatterings, the inter-layer term is not linear in
\mathbf{q}
, but remains finite as
\mathbf{q}\to 0
, leading to a diverging
\mathcal{V}^{(r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})}
.
Nevertheless, as we show in Appendix
B
, if
C^{(r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{YA})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})
is projected to the moiré energy bands, only inter-energy-band elements can remain finite at
\mathbf{q}=0
, while intra-energy-band elements must still vanish linearly with
\mathbf{q}
.
The finite inter-energy-band mixing originates from the re-construction of Bloch wave-functions when the moiré pattern is shifted as a whole.
Here, we will only focus on the intra-energy-band EPC vertices on the flat bands. As will be shown in Sec.
IV.1.3
, when projected to the THF
f
-orbitals, the EPC vertex will be forced by crystalline symmetries (
C_{2z}T
and
C_{3z}
) to vanish linearly as
\mathbf{q}\to 0
.
Since the
f
-orbitals overlap significantly with the flat bands, we will regard the surviving terms as a qualitatively valid estimation for the desired EPC vertex.
However, with crystalline symmetries broken, the above argument can be invalid, and a more careful treatment will be in demand.

For the out-of-plane
\Gamma
-phonons, to the first order of
\mathbf{u}
, none of them changes the
\pi
-bond lengths to induce intra-layer scatterings.
For the
(c,\mathrm{ZA})
mode, neither will it alter the local layer spacing. As a result, only
(c,\mathrm{ZO})
and
(r,\mathrm{ZA}/\mathrm{ZO})
are involved in the EPC vertex by inducing inter-layer scatterings, with amplitudes
\sim\frac{w_{1}}{r_{0}}
. For instance, it is found for
(r,\mathrm{ZA})
modes that (
Section

H.2.3
),

\displaystyle C^{(r,\mathrm{ZA})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=\zeta_{1}~T(\mathbf{r})\ .

(53)

with

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}=\left.\partial_{c}\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{d}_{\parallel}}{\Omega_{g}}~t(\mathbf{d}_{\parallel}+c\hat{\mathbf{z}})e^{-i\eta\mathbf{K}^{(l)}\cdot\mathbf{d}_{\parallel}}\right|_{c=c_{0}}\approx-1.11\frac{w_{1}}{r_{0}}

(54)

Despite the weak scattering amplitude,
\mathcal{V}^{(r,\mathrm{ZA})}=\frac{\theta^{2}}{m_{0}}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{2}u_{0}\zeta_{1}}{\omega_{0,r,\mathrm{ZA}}}\right)^{2}\sim 0.38\mathrm{meV}
is considerable because the phonon frequency
\omega_{\mathbf{q},r,\mathrm{ZA}}=11\mathrm{meV}
is also ultra-low.
In contrast, for
(c/r,\mathrm{ZO})
, due to the much higher
\omega_{\mathbf{q},c/r,\mathrm{ZO}}=100
meV, one can evaluate
\mathcal{V}^{(c/r,\mathrm{ZO})}=\frac{\theta^{2}}{m_{0}}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{2}u_{0}\zeta_{1}}{\omega_{0,c/r,\mathrm{ZO}}}\right)^{2}\sim 0.005\mathrm{meV}
, which will be negligible. Therefore, for general moiré electrons, it suffices to keep
(r,\mathrm{ZA})
among all out-of-plane
\Gamma
-phonons.

For
K
-phonons, it can be shown that, only the
(l,\mathrm{A1})
and
(l,\mathrm{B1})
modes defined in
Eq.

37
contribute to the intra-layer EPC vertex, by altering nearest-neighbor
\pi
-bond lengths (
Section

H.1.3
). The corresponding EPC vertex reads

\displaystyle C^{(l,\mathrm{A1})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{6}v_{F}}{a_{0}r_{0}}\cdot\rho_{l}\sigma_{x}\tau_{x}e^{-i\tau_{z}\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\ ,

(55)

\displaystyle C^{(l,\mathrm{B1})}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})=

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{6}v_{F}}{a_{0}r_{0}}\cdot\rho_{l}\sigma_{x}(-\tau_{y})e^{-i\tau_{z}\mathbf{K}_{l}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\ .

(56)

Here,
\rho_{l}=\frac{\rho_{0}+(-1)^{l-1}\rho_{z}}{2}
is a projector to the layer
l
, and the layer- and valley-dependent phases compensate the discrepancy between the gauge choices of
Eqs.

9
and
38
.

\mathcal{V}^{(l,\mathrm{A1}/\mathrm{B1})}=\frac{\theta^{2}}{m_{0}r_{0}^{2}}\left(\frac{2\sqrt{6}v_{F}/a_{0}}{\omega_{0,c,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO}}}\right)^{2}\sim 4.26\mathrm{meV}
, contributing more significantly than the
(c/r,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO})
modes.
For the inter-layer scatterings, the amplitudes induced by in-plane and out-of-plane
K
-phonons will be
\sim w_{1}|\mathbf{K}^{(l)}|
and
\sim\frac{w_{1}}{r_{0}}
, respectively (
Sections

H.2.2
and
H.2.3
), which are both much smaller than
\frac{v_{F}}{a_{0}r_{0}}
. Due to the high frequency of
K
-phonons, the resultant effective interaction will be negligible.

In obtaining
\hat{H}_{\rm epc}
(
Eq.

43
), all the
O(\theta)
corrections and non-local inter-layer scatterings have been omitted, just as in deriving
\hat{H}_{0}
(
Eq.

4
) (see
Appendix

H
).
\hat{H}_{\rm epc}
enjoys the same space group
P622
and time-reversal symmetry
T
, but
K
-phonons break the independent
\rm U(1)^{2}\times SU(2)^{2}
rotations in two valleys to a global
\rm U(1)\times SU(2)
charge-spin conservation.
We sum up that, for a generic moiré electron captured by the continuum model, its coupling to all the in-plane
\Gamma
-phonons,
(c/r,\mathrm{XA}/\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YA}/\mathrm{YO})
, and out-of-plane
(r,\mathrm{ZA})
, and in-plane
K
-phonon
(l,\mathrm{A1}/\mathrm{B1})
, can be non-negligible, with the effective interaction strength estimated as several milli electron volts.

We also remark that, the EPC strengths presented in this section are
bare
values derived from the tight-binding approach
Eq.

3
. It has been shown that an interplay between EPC and the Coulomb interactions among electrons of energies higher than the phonon frequencies (
\gtrsim\omega_{0,c/r,\mathrm{XO}/\mathrm{YO}}
) can renormalize the phonon-mediated interaction strengths among low-energy electrons away from the bare values
[
77
]
. In particular, Ref.
[
77
]
finds that, to the order of leading logarithmic corrections, the
(l,\mathrm{A1}/\mathrm{B1})
phonon-mediated interaction strengths can be enhanced by a factor around
3.2
, whereas the optical
\Gamma
-phonon-mediated interactions are unchanged.
We will thus introduce two dimensionless RG factors,
\kappa_{\Gamma}
and
\kappa_{K}
, for the overall
\Gamma
-phonon mediated interactions and
K
-phonon mediated interactions, respectively.

IV
Electron-phonon couplings on the
f
-orbitals

As the

e.g.
, heavy Fermi liquid
[
41
,
42
,
43
,

,

]
and symmetry-breaking insulators
[
37
]
, we project the EPC vertices obtained in the last section to the Wannier functions of the
f
-orbitals. Since analyzing the asymptotic
\mathbf{q}
-dependence of the projected vertices will be our main goal, we fix
\kappa_{\Gamma}=\kappa_{K}=1
in this section.

Owing to the small Gaussian width, the overlap between adjacent sites is exponentially small (see
Section

D.1
for discussion on inter-
f
-site EPC vertex). It thus suffices to focus on the on-site transitions induced by phonons,

\displaystyle\hat{H}_{\mathrm{epc}}=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N_{g}}}\sum_{\mathbf{R},s}\sum_{\beta\beta^{\prime}\eta\eta^{\prime}}\sum_{\chi}\sum_{|\mathbf{q}|\lesssim\Lambda}e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{R}}

(57)

\displaystyle~~~~\times u_{\mathbf{q},\chi}\times f^{\dagger}_{\mathbf{R}\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}s}M^{(\chi)}_{\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime},\beta\eta}(\mathbf{q})f_{\mathbf{R}\beta\eta s}

where the projected EPC vertex is

\displaystyle M^{(\chi)}_{\beta\eta,\beta^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=\int\mathrm{d}^{2}\mathbf{r}~e^{i\mathbf{q}\cdot\mathbf{r}}\times

(58)

\displaystyle\left[\sum_{l\alpha,l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}}w^{(\eta)*}_{l\alpha,\beta}(\mathbf{r})~C^{(\chi)}_{l\alpha\eta,l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime}\eta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{q};\mathbf{r})~w^{(\eta^{\prime})}_{l^{\prime}\alpha^{\prime},\beta^{\prime}}(\mathbf{r})\right]

Since
\Gamma
-phonons (

M^{(\chi@\Gamma)}_{\beta\eta,\beta^{\prime}\eta}(\mathbf{q})
(valley off-diagonal elements
M^{(\chi@K)}_{\beta\eta,\beta^{\prime}\overline{\eta}}(\mathbf{q})
) can be non-vanishing.
In below, we will denote such
2\times 2
blocks as
M^{(\chi@\Gamma)}_{\eta,\eta}(\mathbf{q})
(and
M^{(\chi@K)}_{\eta,\overline{\eta}}(\mathbf{q})
), and analyze their long-wave behavior.

M^{(l,\mathrm{A1})}_{0,Re}(\mathbf{q})
and

through
